Location – Current location of the ship which will be either disbanded in a port or in a task force.  If disbanded in an off map port, an “*” is next to the location name.  Clicking on the yellow location centers the Main Map Display on that location.
Below the information columns there are 6 buttons.
    • Change HQ for ships on this list – allows mass reattachment to a different HQ.
    • Ships Withdrawing within 90 days – filters list to display ships due to withdraw within 90 days.
    • Show Ships under repair – displays inactive ships which are undergoing repairs or upgrades.
    • Allow upgrades for ships on this list – sets displayed ships to upgrade automatically.
    • No upgrades for ships on this list – set displayed ships to not upgrade automatically.
    • Show Ships due upgrade – accesses the upgrade ships display screen which provides an overview of ships overdue for or when due to have an upgrade (see section 14.1.1).
Clicking on either of the bottom right Back and Exit buttons returns to the Main Map Display screen.
5.2.6 List all Task Forces Screen (Hotkey T)
This screen provides an overview of all current task forces. The default screen displays only task forces currently located on the Tactical Map.  To view task forces currently located off map there is a button on the bottom of the screen to click on.


The top of the screen has two sets of filters.  The first is a set of task force mission type filters and the second is a yellow filter to Show Ammo/Show Cargo.
From left to right the task force mission type filters are:
All TF – All task forces on the Tactical Map are displayed (default setting).
Air Ops – Air Operations, includes Air Combat and CV Escort task forces.
Surface – Surface Operations, includes Surface Combat, ASW Combat and PT Boat task forces.
Bombard – Bombardment task force.
Fast Trans – Fast Transport task force.
Transport – Transport Operations, includes Transport, Amphibious, Air Transport, Cargo, Tanker, Barge, Landing Craft, Support and Escort task forces.
Replenish – Replenishment task force.
Mine War – Mine warfare operations, includes Minesweeping, Local Minesweeping and Minelaying task forces.
Sub Ops – Submarine Operations, includes Midget Sub Carrier, Midget Submarines, Sub Patrol, Sub Minelaying and Sub Transport task forces.
Beneath the task force type filters there are 14 default plus 5 additional information columns.  Clicking on the yellow column header sorts the column.  From left to right, starting with the default columns, the information shown is:
TF – The Task Force identification number.
Mission – Current mission assigned to the task force (see section 6.1.1.1).  Clicking on the yellow mission name accesses the task force data screen.
Endure – Current average endurance remaining of ships in the task force.
Endure Needed – Current amount of endurance needed to arrive at, and return, from its destination.  The number will be in red if the task force lacks the endurance to reach its destination and return.  Note this calculation does not factor whether the task force can refuel at a waypoint, at the destination or internally from ships with surplus endurance.
Speed Max – The maximum speed in knots the task force can travel.
Speed Cruise – The cruising speed in knots the task force can travel.
Speed Mode – Shows the current speed mode of the task force where M=Mission speed, C=Cruise speed, and F=Full speed.
A/C – Number of aircraft embarked on ships which are able to conduct air operations.  Note these are not aircraft carried as cargo to an overseas destination.
Cargo Supply – Amount of supplies carried as cargo.
Cargo Fuel – Amount of fuel carried as cargo.
Cargo Troops – Number of troops being transported to an overseas destination.
Control – Indicates whether the task force is under Human or Computer control.
Total Ships – Total number of ships in the task force.
Location – Current location of the task force.  If it is docked only the name of the port is displayed.  If it is at its destination port but is not docked, the name of the port followed by “at sea” will be displayed.  If it is yet to arrive at its destination, “move to” is displayed.
Ammo Gun – Percentage of remaining main gun ammunition.
Ammo AAA – Percentage of remaining anti-aircraft weapon ammunition.
Ammo Torp – Percentage of remaining torpedoes.
Ammo ASW – Percentage of remaining anti-submarine weapon ammunition.
Mine – Number of remaining mines.
Below the information columns there are four display filters and one button.  The four display filters are on if green or off if yellow.  From left to right the display filters are:
    • Human – Human controlled task forces are displayed.
    • Computer – Computer controlled task forces are displayed.
    • Computer CS – Continuous Supply Convoy task forces are displayed.
    • Auto Convoy – Auto Convoy task forces are displayed.
Clicking on the bottom button substitutes task forces currently located off map for task forces on the Tactical Map.
Clicking on either of the bottom right Back and Exit buttons returns to the Main Map Display screen.
5.3 Industry Management Screen (Hotkey J)
This screen provides an overview of the player’s war economy plus the capacity to tweak its operations to meet his priorities.  Full details on the operation of the war economy and the limitations on the Allied player are found in section 13.0.
The screen format is the same for both the Allied and Japanese player, but the Japanese player has more opportunities to tweak the war economy by clicking on yellow text. The Industry Management Screen below is for the Japanese side.



The top of the screen has a set of industry filters.  These filters are explained in section 5.1.4.13.1 above.  The filters are on if green and off if yellow.  To the right of the industry filters, is a filter which toggles between displaying all bases and only those bases which have on hand insufficient raw materials to meet fully the input demand for its secondary industry.
Below the filters is a set of 15 information columns.  Some of the columns allow for industry tweaking.  Hovering the curser over the yellow column headers generates a popup which explains what is displayed.  Clicking on the yellow column header sorts the column.  From left to right these columns show:
Base – Name of base.  Clicking on the yellow base name accesses the Base Data screen.
Type – Industry type.  Industry in production is in white.  Industry not in production (aircraft and engine factories in R&D status) is in grey.  Industry in yellow can be adjusted by the Japanese player only.
Upgd – Applies only to aircraft assembly and aircraft engine factories.  Indicates whether the factory is set to upgrade automatically to the next scheduled model.  Clicking on the yellow indicator toggles the upgrade on/off.
Repair – Indicates whether damaged industry at the base is set to repair.  Clicking on the yellow indicator toggles repairs on/off.  Note repair of damaged industry costs supply. 
Prod – Primary industry (manpower, oil, and resources) production cannot be turned off.  Most secondary industry (but not Light Industry or Repair Shipyards) can have its production turned off.  Clicking on the yellow indicator of the eligible secondary industry toggles production on/off.  This option is one of the very few tweaks to his war economy available to the Allied player.
In addition to turning production on/off for eligible secondary industries, clicking on the yellow indicator provides the Japanese player with the following additional options which can be implemented by clicking on the buttons which appear to the right of the scroll bar on the Japanese Industry Management Screen.
    • Industry can be expanded.
    • Aircraft and engine factories can be retooled to another aircraft or engine model respectively.
    • Vehicle factories can be converted to armament factories and vice versa.
    • Merchant shipyards can be converted to naval shipyards and vice versa.
Size-producing – This column shows both the size of the industrial facility and the industrial output of the facility.  The format is undamaged centers, factories or shipyards (damaged centers or factories or shipyards) – industrial outputs would be;
    • MP = Manpower Points.
    • Sup = Supply Points.
    • Res = Resource Points.
    • Oil = Oil Points.
    • Fuel = Fuel Points.
    • HI = Heavy Industry Points.
    • Guns = Armament Points.
    • Veh = Vehicle Points.
    • NavSY pts = Naval Shipyard Points.
    • MerSY pts = Merchant Shipyard Points.
    • Eng/Month = Monthly Engines Production Rate.
    • AC/Month = Monthly Aircraft Production Rate.
In the above screenshot example, Adelaide has a heavy industry facility which is size 40 undamaged heavy industry centers (no damaged heavy industry centers) which produces 80 heavy industry points and 80 supply points 
Failed – A red “X” indicates the facility failed to produce an industrial output last turn.
Requirements Fuel – Amount of fuel points required as an input for the facility to produce output this turn.
Requirements Res – Amount of resource points required as an input for the facility to produce output this turn.
Requirements Oil – Amount of oil points required as an input for the facility to produce output this turn.
Requirements HI – Amount of heavy industry points as an input for the facility to produce output this turn.
At Base Supply – Amount of supply points held at the base.
At Base Fuel – Amount of fuel points held at the base.  The amount is in red if insufficient fuel points are present to produce output this turn.
At Base Res – Amount of resource points held at the base.  The amount is in red if insufficient resource points are present to produce output this turn.
At Base Oil – Amount of oil points held at the base.  The amount is in red if insufficient oil points are present to produce output this turn.
Below the above columns is a second set of information columns which total the information of the first set of information columns.
Proceeding in descending order, the totals shown refer to:
In Pool – The total of the industry output currently in the pools.
Max Prod – Maximum daily output (or monthly production rate for aircraft/engines) if all industry facilities are undamaged.
Disable Prod – Damaged centers/factories/shipyards.
Shut Off Prod – Amount of industry turned off.
Actual Prod – Output from undamaged industry.
Supply Req – Supply points required as industry inputs.
Fuel Req – Fuel points required as industry inputs.
Oil Req – Oil points required as industry inputs.
Res Req – Resource points required as industry inputs.
HI Req – Heavy industry points required as industry points.
Sup Prod – Supply points produced as industry output.
Fuel Prod – Fuel points produced as industry output.
At the bottom of the screen are four buttons to toggle on/off industry facility repairs and production.
Clicking on the bottom right Exit button returns to the Main Map Display.
5.4 Database Screens
AE has several detailed databases.  Editing the databases is only possible through the game editor.  The most important databases (aircraft models, aircraft weapons, ground unit devices, ship classes and ship weapons) are available to be viewed from within the game itself.  These read only databases are first tier information screens which can be easily accessed from the Main Map Display either by clicking on their button on the Menu Bar or pressing their hotkeys.

5.4.1 Plane and Weapon Database (Hotkey D)



This database screen is actually a portal into the following databases:
    • Allied and Japanese aircraft models.
    • Allied and Japanese aircraft weapons.
    • Allied and Japanese ground unit devices.
The default database which is displayed when entering this portal is the enemy aircraft database.  Click on any of the red buttons in the portal to display the other listed databases.
5.4.1.1 Aircraft Database
Two aircraft databases provide in tabular format, summary performance and weapon details on all aircraft models.  Additional information is provided on each listed aircraft model by clicking on the yellow aircraft model name. 
Clicking on the yellow column headers (excluding the Max Alt, Climb and Gun Value headers) sorts the column.  From left to right these columns provide the following summary information;
Aircraft – Name of aircraft model.  Click on the yellow name to access additional information on the aircraft model data screen.
Speed – Maximum speed in mph.  This speed is used in combat.
Cruise – Cruise speed in mph.  This speed is used to get to its destination.  Most relevant for aircraft undertaking naval search or ASW missions.
Max Alt – Maximum altitude in feet, which the model can reach.
Climb - Number of feet per minute it can climb.
Mvr – Maneuver rating.  This is an important metric in air-air combat between fighters. Fighters, fighter bombers, night fighters, float fighters and floatplanes have 5 different maneuver ratings to match 5 different altitude bands.  Generally, as altitude increases the maneuver rating decreases.  This additional information is seen in the aircraft model data screen.  For bombers the maneuver rating is an important metric in the flak algorithms.
Durab – Durability rating.  This is an indicator as to how susceptible the aircraft is to combat damage.  It is also a metric factored into the model’s maintenance requirements.
Armor – An armor rating typically represents the presence of self-sealing fuel tanks and cockpit armor.  A rating of 0 means the aircraft model has no armor, whereas a rating of 1 means the aircraft model has armor.  A limited number of aircraft models were optimized for ground close air support and these models have an armor rating of 2.  Armor provides enhanced protection for the pilot and survivability for the airframe.
Endur – Distance (measured in minutes) the aircraft can fly on its internal fuel tanks.  Some models can use external drop tanks to increase the distance.  Details on drop tank distances are found on the aircraft model data screen.
Load – Load in pounds of ordnance which can be carried.  The aircraft model data screen lists the actual ordnance which is carried.  This metric is important in determining whether a heavy, medium or attack bomber is flying from too small an airfield (see section 9.4).  For aircraft undertaking a transport mission it determines how many supply points or troops can be carried (see section 7.2.4).
Gun Value – Quantitative measure of the caliber and number of guns carried.  It is not indicative of the quality of the guns nor does it reflect their location.  The aircraft model data screen provides information on the quality and location of the guns.
Type - Aircraft type.
Nation – Nationality which produces and generally flies this aircraft model.
Click the red Exit button at the bottom right to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.1.2 Aircraft Model Data Screen
This is a second tier information screen which is accessed by clicking on the yellow aircraft model name in the Aircraft Database.  This screen provides very important additional information such as:
    • Whether the aircraft model can operate from aircraft carriers.
    • Specific weapon details.
    • Flying ranges in hexes without and with (if applicable) drop tanks.
    • The five maneuver ratings for fighters/fighter bombers/night fighters/float fighters/floatplanes.
    • Service rating.
    • Availability date.
The yellow column headers are exactly the same as those explained in 5.4.1.1.
Below the yellow aircraft model name “Carrier capable” is in white for models which can operate from aircraft carriers.  Models which do not have this indicator under their name cannot operate from aircraft carriers even if they equip air units which are rated as either “carrier trained” or “carrier capable” air units.  Other identifiers in white below the yellow aircraft model name are whether the model is float-equipped and if a level bomber, the sub-type of level bomber (heavy, medium, light or attack).
Click on the yellow name to return to the Aircraft Database screen.
From left to right the aircraft guns field presents the following weapon data;
    • Name of weapon.
    • Number of weapons.
    • Location of the weapon where C = centerline, F = forward facing, TR = top rear facing, BR = bottom rear facing, TT = top turret, BT = bottom turret, R = rear facing (tail gun), S = side.  Unless it is a turret, a gun is only useable in its field of fire.
    • Type of weapon.
    • Effect rating of weapon.
    • Penetration rating of weapon.
    • Range in feet.
    • Weapon accuracy (takes into account the location).
For aircraft models equipped with an electronics device, the name of the electronic device is shown below the aircraft guns field.
The load field data is broken down into maximum (normal) and reduced ordnance capacity.  The maximum ordnance capacity applies only when the aircraft operates within its normal range.  The reduced ordnance capacity applies when the aircraft operates between its normal and extended range.  Some aircraft types will fly with their reduced ordnance capacity instead of their maximum ordnance under certain circumstances.  In this field, C = centerline, INT = Internal bomb bay and XT = external hard point.
Note that use of centerline drop tanks will replace any centerline ordnance.
The engine field shows how many engines are used by the model.
The range field shows the maximum, normal and extended ranges (with and without drop tanks) in miles and hexes.
    • Maximum range is the one way distance.  This is primarily used for ferrying from one airfield to another airfield but is also relevant for transport missions.
    • Normal range is the range to target and back which the model can fly with maximum ordnance.
    • Extended range exceeds normal range and is the range to target and back with reduced ordnance.  In addition to the ordnance penalty, flying at extended range increases pilot fatigue, operational losses and the chances of the aircraft needing to return to base early due to fuel limits.
Fighters, fighter bombers, night fighters, float fighters and floatplanes display below their range field, their five altitude bands (in feet) and associated maneuver ratings.  The indicated maneuver rating applies to any altitude within the altitude band.  In the above example, the A6M2 Zero has a maneuver rating of 27 when flying between 16 and 20,000 feet but the rating drops to 21 when it flies between 21 and 31,000 feet.
Not shown in the above screenshot is the Service Rating of the aircraft model and the availability date.  The Service Rating is a number between 1 and 5 (inclusive) which indicates the models complexity (higher rating = greater complexity) and ease of maintenance.  The service rating and the durability rating are the key factors in determining how regularly maintenance needs to be performed on the model.  The service rating is also one of the factors taken into account in determining the time needed for repairing damage. 
The availability date shows when the aircraft model enters service.  For an Allied aircraft this is a fixed date, except for any models which are solely produced by on map factories and have had their upgrade turned off by the Allied player.  For Japanese aircraft, the availability date represents the current estimated introduction into service date.  The estimate is based on the current RD factories devoted to researching that model and engine used.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.1.3 Aircraft Weapons Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Aircraft Weapons button in the Plane and Weapons Devices Database portal.
The Aircraft Weapons Database shows all Allied and Japanese items which can equip their aircraft.  Items shown include:
    • Machine guns
    • Cannon
    • Bombs
    • Rockets
    • Electronic devices (includes radar and cameras)
    • Drop tanks
Note this database does not identify whether the aircraft weapon is Allied or Japanese. 
Clicking on the yellow column headers (except Avail) sorts the column.  From left to right the columns provide the following information.
Aircraft Weapon – Name of the weapon which also identifies the type.
A/Arm – Maximum damage possible against hardened (armored) ground devices.
A/Soft – Maximum damage possible against soft skinned (non-armored) ground devices.
Range – For machine guns, cannons and rockets the optimized convergence distance (measured in 100 yards) to the target.  For radars range is not an indication of distance but of additional chances to detect enemy raids.
Penetration – Value checked against ship armor rating.  The aircraft weapon penetration value must exceed the ship armor rating for a penetrating hit to result.
Effect – Maximum possible damage from a hit against a ship.  For drop tanks it is the size of the drop tank.
Load Cost – Ship cargo space needed to transport 1 item of this device.  If static the weapon cannot be moved and usually is found only on a ship or Coastal Defense (CD) unit.
Avail – Date available.  Note for drop tanks this date is superseded by the historical date each aircraft model was equipped with the drop tank.  That date can be seen by launching the editor and opening the scenario.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.

5.4.1.4 Anti-Aircraft Weapons Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Anti-Aircraft Weapons button in the Aircraft and Ground Devices Database portal.
The Anti-Aircraft Weapons Database shows all Allied and Japanese land and naval devices designed to fire against enemy aircraft.  Two types of devices are shown in this database; AAA guns which operate primarily against aircraft but can have a limited secondary use against other targets, and DP (Duel Purpose) guns which are designed for dual use.
Most of the DP guns and some of the AAA guns shown in this database are deployed primarily on ships and those devices can be found in the Naval Database portal as well (see section 5.4.2).
Note this database does not identify whether the aircraft weapon is Allied or Japanese.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right the columns provide the following information;
Anti-Aircraft – Name of the device which also identifies the type.
Effect – Maximum possible damage from a hit.
Ceiling – Maximum altitude which the weapon can reach.  There is a very small possibility that an enemy aircraft which flies just above this ceiling will be hit.
Load Cost – Ship cargo space needed to transport one item of this device.  It is also important in determining whether the device can be transported by air.  If static the weapon cannot be moved and usually is found only on a ship or CD unit.
Type – Indicates whether it is an AAA gun or a DP gun.
Avail – Date available.
Upgrades to – Scheduled to upgrade to this device.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.1.5 Artillery Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Artillery button in the Aircraft and Ground Devices Database portal.
The Artillery Weapons Database shows all Allied and Japanese army weapon, naval gun and DP gun types.  These weapon types are found in ground units and ships, and are used as appropriate against land or naval targets.
Most of the DP gun and naval gun types shown in this database are deployed primarily on ships and can also be found in a similar database accessed through the Naval Database portal as well (see section 5.4.2).
Note this database does not identify whether the artillery weapon is Allied or Japanese.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right the columns provide the following information.
Artillery – Name of device.
A/Arm – Maximum damage possible against hardened (armored) ground devices.
A/Soft – Maximum damage possible against soft skinned (non-armored) ground devices.
Range – Horizontal distance to the ground or naval target.  Note, for DP guns only the horizontal range to a ground or naval target is shown.  The vertical altitude ceiling against aircraft is shown in the Anti-Aircraft Weapons Database (section 5.4.1.3).  Range also determines whether an army weapon or DP gun type participates in land bombardment attacks or shoots at a ship.
Load Cost – Ship cargo space needed to transport 1 item of this device.  It is also important in determining whether the device can be transported by air.  Devices with a static load cost are found either on a ship or in a ground unit where the scenario designer’s intention is to make the ground unit permanently immobile.
Type – Artillery can be one of army weapon, naval gun or DP gun.  The type is factored into the combat algorithms.
Avail – Date the weapon becomes available.
Upgrades to – Scheduled to upgrade to this device.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.1.6 Vehicles Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Vehicles button in the Aircraft and Ground Devices Database portal.
The Vehicles Database shows all Allied and Japanese vehicle devices found in ground units.  It includes:
    • Tanks
    • Armored cars
    • Half tracks
    • Tractors
    • Engineer vehicles
    • Motorized support
Note this database does not identify whether the vehicle device is Allied or Japanese.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right the columns provide the following information;
Vehicle – Name of device.
A/Arm – Maximum damage possible against hardened (armored) ground devices.
A/Soft – Maximum damage possible against soft skinned (non-armored) ground devices.
Range – Distance to the target.
Armor – Armor defense value.  In combat this rating is assessed against the offensive A/Arm rating of enemy weapons.
Load Cost – Ship cargo space needed to transport 1 item of this device.  It is also important in determining whether the device can be transported by air.  The two “vehicles” which are listed as having a static load cost are special items which are found in the periodic special convoys which arrive at Allied off map bases.
Type – Tanks are an AFV type, all other vehicles are a vehicle type.
Avail – Date of availability.
Upgrades to – Scheduled to upgrade to this device.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.1.7 Infantry Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Infantry button in the Aircraft and Ground Devices Database portal.
The Infantry Database shows all Allied and Japanese infantry and engineer squads found in ground units.  Essentially these devices are your foot fighting troops.  For game purposes cavalry squads, specialist non mechanized troops (such as paratroopers, raiders, and commandos) and support troops are considered to be foot soldiers similar to infantry.
Note this database does not identify whether the infantry device is Allied or Japanese.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right the columns provide the following information;
Infantry – Name of device.  Engineer devices which include “Cmbt” in the device name are Combat Engineers who have a special role to play in combat.
A/Arm – Maximum damage possible against hardened (armored) ground devices.
A/Soft – Maximum damage possible against soft skinned (non-armored) ground devices.
Load Cost – Ship cargo space needed to transport 1 item of this device.  It is also important in determining whether the device can be transported by air.  Devices with a static load cost are found in ground units where the scenario designer’s intention is to make the ground unit permanently immobile.
Type –Either squad or engineer.  The former includes all infantry like devices (such as infantry, cavalry, paratrooper, support) and the latter combat and non-combat engineers.  Both types are soft skinned ground devices.
Avail – Date of availability.
Upgrades to – Scheduled to upgrade to this device.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.1.8 Electronics Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Electronics button in the Aircraft and Ground Devices Database portal.
The Electronics Database shows all Allied and Japanese electronic devices.  It includes:
    • Air Search radar – Ground or ship based radar which detects enemy aircraft.
    • Search radar – Ship based radar which detects enemy ships.
    • Aircraft radar – Airborne radar which detects either enemy aircraft or ships.
    • ELINT – In game any intelligence gathering device.
Note this database does not identify whether the electronics is Allied or Japanese except where the same device name is used.  Where devices are similarly named there is a nationality identifier in brackets after the name, where (J) = Japanese, (A) = Allied, (C) = Chinese and (LL) = Chinese Lend Lease.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right the columns provide the following information.
Name – Name of device.  For similarly named devices there is a nationality identifier at the end of the name where (J) = Japanese, (A) = Allied, (C) = Chinese and (LL) = Chinese Lend Lease.
Range – In the game radar does not extend beyond the hex it is located.  The range number therefore does not indicate the distance the device operates.  In AE radar range is used by the detection algorithms to determine how many detection chances.  The greater the range the more chances and earlier detection information is provided.
Acc – Accuracy of the device.
Effect – The higher the listed effect value, the more detailed the detected information will be.
Type – Type of radar.  Air Search is land or ship based used to detect aircraft, Search Radar is ship based used to detect ships, A/C Radar is aircraft based used to detect either aircraft or ships, ELINT = camera.
Load Cost – For ground based radar devices this number indicates the ship cargo space needed to transport 1 item of this device.  Ship based radars have no load cost in this column.
Upgrades to – Scheduled to upgrade to this device.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.

5.4.2 Naval Database (Hotkey V)
This database screen is actually a portal into the following databases:
    • Allied and Japanese ship classes.
    • Allied and Japanese ship weapons.



The default database which is displayed when entering this portal is your own ship class database. Click on any of the red buttons in the portal to display the other listed databases.
5.4.2.1 Ship Class Database
Two ship databases provide in tabular format, summary performance and weapon details on all ship classes.  Additional information is provided on each listed ship class by clicking on the yellow ship class name (see section 5.4.2.1.1).
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right these columns provide the following summary information;
Type – Ship types (see section 20.1.2 for the different ship types).
Ship – Name of ship class.  The ship class name incorporates the month/year of a scheduled upgrade.  Hovering the curser over the ship class name generates a popup which indicates the type of ship class it belongs to.  Click on the yellow ship class name to access additional information on the ship class data screen (see section 5.4.2.1.1).
Speed – Maximum speed in knots.  This can be translated into hexes travelled per phase at the rate of (speed x 12 /40). 
Cruise – Cruise speed in knots.  This can be translated into hexes travelled per phase at the rate of (speed x 12 /40).  
Endure – Ship endurance.  This is the number of nautical miles at cruise speed ships of this class can travel with full bunkers.  This can be translated into hexes at the rate (endurance/40).  
Fuel – Amount of fuel points held in the bunkers when they are full.
Mvr – Maneuver rating of the ship class.  The higher the value the greater the ability to dodge enemy bombs and torpedoes.
Durab – Durability rating of the ship class.  The durability rating is used in several areas:
    • For all vessels it is an overall measure of the vessels seaworthiness.
    • For submarines it is also the maximum diving depth in feet, determined by (durability x 10).
    • Victory Points awarded for a sunk vessel is partly determined on the durability rating.
    • It is also factored into the Japanese ship building program.
Belt – Thickness in millimeters of the hull armor at and below the waterline.
Deck – Thickness in millimeters of the deck armor.
Tower – Thickness in millimeters of the ships superstructure armor.
Capacity – For combat ship classes, represents the number of aircraft which can operate from the ship.  For non-combat ship classes, represents the total troop, cargo and bulk liquid carrying capacity.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.2.1.1 Ship Class Data Screen
This is a second tier information screen which is accessed by clicking on the yellow ship class name in the Ship Class Database.  This screen provides very important additional information on the ship class weapons such as:
    • Number in total and per mount.
    • Weapon facing, type and characteristics.
    • Ammunition quantity per weapon.
    • Type and function of electronic devices.

The yellow column headers are exactly the same as those explained in section 5.4.2.1.  Beneath the yellow columns are 10 white columns which contain the following additional information;
Weapon – Name of weapon device.
Num – Total number of barrels of that weapon device.
Face – Facing of the weapon.  It indicates the direction the weapon is facing, where F = forward, R = Rear, LS = left side (or port), RS = right side (or starboard), C = center and A = all sides.  Facing is very important as weapons can only shoot in the arc of their facing.
Mount – For ships, the mount or turret is the basic unit (not the individual barrel) that can shoot or be damaged.  The number shown here indicates how many weapon barrels are grouped together in each mount (or turret).  The above screenshot shows the Iowa 4/43 ship class as having 2 forward facing mounts with 3x 16in barrels in each mount and 1 single rear facing mount with 3x16in barrels.
Armor – Thickness in millimeters of the armor for that mount (or turret).  Turrets are usually heavily armored whereas mounts usually have no armor or only a thin shield for protection.
Ammo – Number of combat rounds allocated for that mount.
Range – Maximum horizontal range in yards for combat against ships or ground units.  An “*” indicates the weapon can also shoot at aircraft.  Note, for weapons shown with an “*”, only the horizontal range to a ground or naval target is shown.  The vertical altitude ceiling against aircraft is shown in the Anti-Aircraft Weapons Database (see sections 5.4.1.3 or 5.4.2.3) or the Dual Purpose Gun Database (section 5.4.2.4).
Effect – Maximum possible damage which might be inflicted on the enemy by a penetrating hit.  This rating is also used to determine rearming costs and consumption of operational points.
Penetration – Maximum value of enemy defensive armor which this weapon device is able to penetrate.  If the penetration value is less than the enemy armor defensive value only non-penetrating hits are possible.  Note a hit against a listed armor value which is less than the listed penetration value does not automatically result in a penetrating hit. 
Type – Type of weapon device.
Details on the electronics suite carried by the ship class are shown below the weapons data.  Unlike aircraft or ground units, ships will usually have two radar sets, one to detect enemy aircraft and another to detect enemy ships.  The radar range shown indicates how many additional chances to detect the enemy with the greater the range the more chances.  The higher the effect number shown, the better quality is the returned detection information.  Details on the operation and limitations of radar, is found in section 10.3.
Click on the yellow name to return to the Aircraft Database screen.  Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.2.2 Electronics Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Electronics button in the Ship Class Database portal.
There are only two differences between accessing the Electronics Database through the Aircraft and Ground Devices Database portal (see section 5.4.1.7 above) or the Ship Class Database portal.  The main difference is the one displayed through the Ship Class Database portal excludes electronic devices which are carried only by aircraft.  The second difference is there is no “Upgrade to“ column shown.  Otherwise, the format and information is the same.
5.4.2.3 Anti-Aircraft Guns Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Anti-Aircraft Guns button in the Ship Class Database portal.
Accessing the Anti-Aircraft Guns Database through the Ship Class Database portal brings up a truncated version of the same database which is accessed through the Aircraft and Ground Devices Databases portal (see section 5.4.1.3).  The differences are that anti-aircraft guns used only by ground units are not shown in the truncated version, and the truncated version does not display, Type, Availability or Upgrade information, instead showing a column with a header titled “Rate of Fire”.  
Please note that the “Rate of Fire” column shown in the truncated version does not represent Rate of Fire.  It is a variable value which is used in different internal game algorithms.  Other than these differences, the format and information of the two versions is the same.
5.4.2.4 Dual Purpose Guns Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Dual Purpose Guns button in the Ship Class Database portal.
The Dual Purpose Guns Database is a truncated version of the type Dual Purpose guns shown in the Artillery Database (section 5.4.1.4) in that Dual Purpose guns which are only found in ground units are not shown here.
Ship based Dual Purpose guns have two equally important roles.  They are used, similar as Naval Guns, to fire against naval targets (primarily enemy ships but also in bombardments of coastal areas) and similar to Anti-Aircraft Guns, to shoot at enemy aircraft.
Note this database does not identify whether the dual purpose gun weapon is Allied or Japanese.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right these columns provide the following information;
DP Guns – Name of the device.
Penetration - Maximum value of enemy armor which this weapon device is able to penetrate.  If the penetration value listed is less than the listed armor value, only non-penetrating hits are possible.  Note a hit against a listed armor value which is less than the listed penetration value does not automatically result in a penetrating hit.
Effect - Maximum possible damage which might be inflicted on the enemy by a penetrating hit.  This rating is also used to determine ship rearming costs and consumption of operational points.
Range - Maximum range in yards for combat against ships or ground targets.
Ceiling - Maximum altitude which the weapon can reach.  There is a very small possibility that an enemy aircraft which flies just above this ceiling will be hit.
Rate of Fire – This is not the Rate of Fire of the device.  It is a variable value which is used in different internal game algorithms.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.2.5 Naval Guns Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Naval Guns button in the Ship Class Database portal.
Naval Guns lack the elevation range to be an effective anti-aircraft weapon system.  Traditionally they were the big main armament of capital ships.  Their purpose is to shoot at enemy ships or coastal ground targets, usually in support of invasions.
Note this database does not identify whether the dual purpose gun weapon is Allied or Japanese.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right these columns provide the following information;
Naval Guns – Name of device.
Penetration - Maximum value of enemy armor which this weapon device is able to penetrate.  If the penetration value listed is less than the listed armor value, only non-penetrating hits are possible.  Note a hit against a listed armor value which is less than the listed penetration value does not automatically result in a penetrating hit. 
Effect - Maximum possible damage which might be inflicted on the enemy by a penetrating hit.  This rating is also used to determine ship rearming costs and consumption of operational points.
Range - Maximum range in yards for combat against ships or ground targets.
Rate of Fire - This is not the Rate of Fire of the device.  It is a variable value which is used in different internal game algorithms.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.2.6 ASW Weapons Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the ASW Weapons button in the Ship Class Database portal.
The ASW Weapons Database shows all Allied and Japanese anti-submarine weapon devices used by ships.  There are no air dropped ASW devices in the game.
Note this database does not identify whether the dual purpose gun weapon is Allied or Japanese.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right these columns provide the following information;
ASW Weapons – Name of device.
Effect - Maximum possible damage which might be inflicted on the enemy by a penetrating hit.  Unlike most other ship weapons this rating is not used to determine ship rearming costs and consumption of operational points.
Depth – Depth in feet the ASW device can normally reach to make a hit on a submarine.  Note there is a chance a submarine with a deeper diving depth than the ASW device will still be hit.
Accuracy – A variable which is factored into the ASW combat routines.
Load Cost - Value used to determine;
    • Minimum size of port needed for reloading the ASW device.
    • Rearming costs.
    • Consumption of operation points.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.
5.4.2.7 Torpedoes Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Torpedoes button in the Ship Class Database portal.
The Torpedoes Database shows all Allied and Japanese torpedoes used by ships or aircraft.  It does not identify which side uses the device or whether it equips a ship or aircraft.
Torpedo dud rates are not shown in-game, but they can be seen if the scenario is loaded into the editor.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right these columns provide the following information;
Torpedoes – Name of device.
Effect - Maximum possible damage which might be inflicted on the enemy by a hit which detonates.  Unlike most other ship weapons this rating is not used to determine ship rearming costs and consumption of operational points.
Range - Maximum range in yards for combat against ships.
Speed – Speed in knots.
Avail – Date when torpedo becomes available in format year/month.  If no date shown the torpedo is available from the start of the scenario.
Load Cost - Value used to determine; 
    • Minimum size of port needed for reloading the torpedo device.
    • Rearming costs.
    • Consumption of operational points.
Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.

5.4.2.8 Mines Database
This database is accessed by clicking on the Mines button in the Ship Class Database portal.
The Mines Database shows all Allied and Japanese mines which are dropped by ships or aircraft.  It does not identify which side uses the device or who drops it.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  From left to right these columns provide the following information;
Mines – Name of device.
Effect - Maximum possible damage which might be inflicted on the enemy by a hit.  Unlike most other ship weapons this rating is not used to determine ship rearming costs and consumption of operational points.
Accuracy - A variable which is factored into the mine combat routines.
Avail - Date when mine enters into production in format year/month.  If no date shown the mine is available from the start of the scenario.
Load Cost - Value used to determine; 
    • Minimum size of port needed for reloading the mine device.
    • Rearming costs.
    • Consumption of operational points.

Click on the bottom red Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.

5.5. Signal Reports Screen (Hotkey L)
This screen is accessed by clicking on the Signal Reports button on the Menu Bar at the top of the Main Map Display or by pressing the “L” hotkey.



The Signal Reports screen displays information derived from interception of enemy communications.  Only interceptions of last turn enemy communications are displayed on the in-game screen but if the “-archive” command switch (see section 1.3.1.2) is used, a complete record of all Signal Reports is available in the Archive sub-folder found within the “Saves” sub-directory of your game installation.
Allied Signal is usually more extensive than Japanese Signal.  This reflects to a certain degree the different historical outcomes for both sides of their respective intelligence gathering efforts.  While this would appear to provide the Allied player with an advantage in the game, the opportunity exists for a wily Japanese player to create false intelligence intended to mislead their Allied opponent. 
Special note for PBEM games.  No Signal Reports are saved for the Allied player as the replay save is performed at the start of the turn before Signal reports are generated.  The Allied player needs to run the Combat Replay save to create the report for the last end of turn resolution.  No Signal Reports are stored in a PBEM save in order that the other player does not gain a benefit by examining the reports created for the other player.
The Signal Report usually provides intelligence in these four areas:
    • Location of enemy task forces (radio transmissions detected at ...).
    • Location of individual ground units (unit name located at ...).
    • Future enemy ground objectives (unit name is planning for an attack on ...).
    • Ground unit’s overseas destinations (unit name loaded on ship name moving to ...).

Click on the top left Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.

5.6 Operational Report Screen (Hotkey O)
This screen is accessed by clicking on the Operational Report button on the Menu Bar at the top of the Main Map Display or by pressing the “O” hotkey.



The Operational Report is essentially a repetition of all messages which were flashed on the lower left of the screen during the last end of turn computer resolution phase.  If the “-archive” command switch (see section 1.3.1.2) is used, a complete record of all Operational Reports is available in the Archive sub-folder found within the “Saves” sub-directory of your game installation.
For PBEM games the Allied player needs to run the Combat Replay save to create the report for the last end of turn resolution.  No Operational Reports are stored in a PBEM save in order that the other player does not gain a benefit by examining the reports created for the other player.
The range of information displayed in the Operational report includes:
    • Coastwatcher reports (see section 8.7).
    • Notification of friendly and enemy air search and recon over task forces, bases, ground units.
    • Task force destination arrivals.
    • Ship repair completions.
    • Task forces short of fuel to complete mission.
    • Ship sinking reports.
    • Ships and submarines returning to their assigned patrol zones.
    • Aircraft grounded for maintenance.
    • Aircraft crash landings.
    • Aircraft transport missions.
    • Fate of pilot aces.
    • Pilot promotions and kills achieved last turn.
    • Replacements and upgrades.
    • Unit disbands and withdrawals (includes penalty for overdue units).
    • Delay on aircraft returning to pools.
    • Arrival of ground, air and ship reinforcements.
    • Base captures.
    • Partisan attacks from under garrisoned bases.
    • Base building achieving new level (occasionally enemy base building information is shown).
 
Click on the top left Exit button to return to the Main Map Display.

5.7 Auto-convoy System Screen (Hotkey H)
This screen is accessed by clicking on the Auto-convoy System button on the Menu Bar at the top of the Main Map Display or by pressing the “H” hotkey.



The Auto-convoy Screen allows for the logistical requirements of overseas bases with port facilities, to be handled by the computer within the parameters set by the player.  How to set those parameters and how auto-convoys operate, is detailed in section 15.5.
Clicking on the yellow column headers sorts the column.  Hovering the curser over some of the column headers generates a popup and identifies a right-click option.  From left to right these columns provide the following information;
Auto Convoy – Identifies whether the base participates in the auto-convoy system.  Click on the yellow no/yes to toggle the participation.
Name – Name of base with port facilities.  Allied base names with an “*” are supplied from the Western Supply Base if participating in the auto-convoy system.  Click on the yellow base name to toggle on/off its participation.
Supplies – Quantity of supply points currently located at the base.
Supplies Required – (default) Quantity of supply points which are required to meet current tempo of operations.  The number is in red if less than <1x supplies required, and in yellow if <2x supplies required.  If “+” is next to the quantity, the player has manually increased the supply requirement at the base.  If the yellow column header is right-clicked, the display changes to show the number of days left before the existing supplies are consumed. 
Fuel – Quantity of fuel points currently located at the base.
Fuel Required - (default) Quantity of fuel points which are required to meet current fuel demand.  The number is in red or orange if less than <1x fuel required, and in yellow if <2x fuel required.  If “+” is next to the quantity, there are currently ships disbanded in the port.  If the yellow column header is right-clicked, the display changes to show the number of days left before the existing fuel is consumed.
Port – Size of port.
AF – Size of airfield.
Location – Hex coordinates of the base.
Below the information columns are several toggles which are used to filter the display to show participating Allied bases which are serviced by the Western or Eastern Supply Base.  Japanese bases which participate in the auto-convoy system are all serviced from the Osaka/Kyoto Supply Base.
To the right of the scroll bar is a set of toggles to display ships currently in the Supply Bases.  Clicking on the Supply Base toggle changes the display from bases eligible to participate in the Auto-convoy System to the eligible ships (cargo, tanker and escorts) disbanded in the Supply Base.  
Full details on the operation of the auto-convoy system are provided in section 15.5.
6.0 Naval Units
Naval units, the backbone of the Pacific campaign, carried the brunt of the conflict on their steel shoulders. They took on a wide array of Missions that were as diverse as the tasks they were built for; from the largest battleship to the smallest PT boat, each served a purpose and each contributed their part to the war effort. Combat notwithstanding, the unseen yet vital services of logistics were almost exclusively carried across the water. Whoever rules the waves controls the means of supplying their troops on land, which ties in to success in the air and on the ground.
6.1 Task Forces and Ships
Though War in the Pacific, Admiral’s Edition™ accounts for individual ships, they are not represented on the map unless they are part of a Task Force (hereafter referred to as TF). If they are anchored at a port, you can find them by clicking on an anchor symbol next to a base or by selecting them from the List All Ships Screen in the HCD. When a TF is docked in a port, its ships are still listed as part of the TF.
There are four main types of ships:
    • Combat (surface) ships (Battleships, Aircraft Carriers, Destroyers, etc.). Surface ship task forces can execute a wide variety of Missions, such as launching carrier air strikes, bombarding ground targets and laying or sweeping minefields.
    • Submarines, usually sent on patrol as a TF, usually with only one sub in each. The computer can give it patrol orders, or you can choose to give it a Destination Hex (DH) yourself. Missions for sub TF’s are Sub Patrol, Sub Minelaying, Sub Transport, Midget Sub, and Midget Sub Carrier (refer to section 6.1.1.1 for more details). Subs can attack enemy shipping when they occupy the same hex.
    • Transports
    • Auxiliaries such as oilers, mine sweepers, mine layers, etc.
A listing of ship types is provided in section 20.1.2. 
Ships begin scenarios either in TFs, or “at anchor” in a port. Ships that are at anchor will not move, but they will defend the base if attacked, adding half their AA firepower to the base in defense of the port.
6.1.1 Task Force Symbols
In War in the Pacific, Admiral Edition™, the TF symbols on the game map vary in appearance, based on the type of Mission it currently has assigned. For a list of these on map symbols, refer to section 4.2.2.5.  
6.1.1.1 Task Force Missions
    • Air Combat. The vanguard of all naval offense had at least one Aircraft Carrier (or ‘flattop’) with it, projecting strength through their air components. These Missions seek to destroy the enemy in any form wherever he may be found – but especially sought out enemy flattops.   Maximum Task Force size is 25 ships.
    • Surface Combat. When air power fails, or a more ‘personal’ touch is required, these TF’s serve to allow battlewagons (Battleships, Cruisers, and Destroyers, as well as other specialist vessels) to seek out and destroy enemy ships.  Maximum of 25 ships.
    • Bombardment. These TFs differ from Surface Combat TFs in that the assigned ships’ big guns are destined to shell enemy-held bases, facilities, and troop concentrations.  Also limited to 25 ships.
    • Fast Transport. These TFs revolve around the transport of supplies and troops, but in faster, more agile vessels (such as converted Destroyers). However, these ships cannot carry payloads anywhere near the size of regular Transports.  Maximum of 25 ships.
    • Transport. These TFs are tasked with moving vital supplies and ground troops to the ever-thirsty front lines. Troops and supplies are loaded for maximum efficiency and do not arrive battle ready.   There may be up to 100 ships in transport TFs.
    • Replenishment. Vital Missions unto themselves, without which attack TFs would become little more than floating airstrips and pillboxes for want of the precious fuel, oil, ammunition, and supplies. These specialty vessels carry these exclusively for the fleets they operate with.  Maximum TF size is 25 ships.
    • Mine Laying. These task group Missions seek to lay the silent killers of the seas – anti-ship mines. A well-placed minefield can cripple a mighty task group, but minefields degrade over time, so these specialist ships can also maintain already laid minefields.   Maximum TF size is 25 ships.
    • Sub Patrol. These (preferably) unseen and unheard task forces, usually comprised of a single submarine each, gather vital intelligence and take out enemy targets of opportunity.   There may be up to 25 ships in a Submarine Patrol TF, but one is usual.
    • Sub Minelaying. Stealthier than their surface-bound cousins, a submarine mine laying task force can lay mines quietly, but in numbers more limited than Mine Warfare surface groups.  Maximum of 25 submarines capable of laying mines per TF.
    • Sub Transport. Like Minelayers, these task force Missions are harder to detect than that of Transports or Fast Transports, but their capacity is limited when even when compared to Fast Transports.  Maximum of 25 ships per TFs.
    • Cargo. These TFs are meant to carry supplies and resources.  Maximum of 100 ships per TF.
    • Barge. These TFs are tasked with moving supplies and ground troops in the front line areas over limited distances. They are slow and plodding, and carry a minimal quantity of war materiel, but they can be useful in restricted areas.  Task Force size is limited to 25 ships.
    • Air Transport. A variation of the Cargo TF, in which CVEs are used for carrying aircraft cargo instead of operational air groups.  The AI will only form this type of TF when it has CVEs without air groups.  Maximum of 25 ships per TF.
    • CV Escort. A covering force for transport TFs that sweep the seas of those pesky surface raiders and submarines.  This is a variation of Air Combat, just with smaller/slower carriers, and is limited to 25 ships.
    • Amphibious.  These TFs are used for invasions of enemy held locations.  They carry troops and supplies in “Combat Load”, which is less efficient than commercial loading.  Maximum TF size is 100 ships.
    • Anti-Submarine Warfare (ASW) These TFs are used for hunting enemy submarines exclusively and will not allow large warships in them (only AM, DMS, SC, PG, PC, APD, DE, DD type ships). The chance of this TF contacting enemy subs in coastal hexes is higher when compared to open water hexes. ASW TFs will get a better chance of shooting first if a contact is made.  ASW TFs are limited to 4 ships.
    • PT Boat. These TFs serve to protect the ports are assigned to by patrolling for, and reacting to, enemy surface forces or bombardment TFs.  Maximum size is 16 PT boats.
    • Tanker. A TF meant to carry fuel or oil.  Maximum TF size is 25 ships.
    • Mine Sweeping. These task group Missions seek to find and remove the silent killers of the seas – anti-ship mines.   Maximum size is 25 ships.
    • Landing Craft. A merger of Barge and Amphibious; a beaching-craft invasion TF primarily used for shorter range and even beach-to-beach invasions.  Maximum TF size is 100 ships
    • Support. These TFs contain Repair ships, Tenders, Fuel and Ammunition ships and their escort. These TF can move to forward locations and create a temporary Naval base.  Also useful for moving support ships between rear area bases.  Maximum of 36 ships in each Task Force.
    • Local Mine Sweeping. These task group Missions seek to find and remove anti-ship mines in a localized area.  The ships that make up these TFs are generally not deep sea capable.  Maximum of 4 ships in each TF.
    • Escort. These are general purpose “ship movement” TFs. They are used to evacuate damaged ships from the battle area, and to move ships between bases.  They cannot load or unload, or perform any other function, and will flee all enemy forces.  They may include any type of surface ship, including those too badly damaged to fight.   Maximum TF size is 100 ships.
    • Midget Submarine.  A subset of Sub Patrol TFs, that are available only to the Japanese.  These small subs have very limited range, but can be used to protect bases. They can also be used in conjunction with a Midget Sub carrier to attack enemy bases.  Limited to 4 midget subs per TF.
    • Midget Sub Carrier.  Certain Japanese (only) submarines were configured to carry midget subs into combat.  These TFs require both a suitable carrier sub and an available Midget Sub to combine into the TF.  TF must contain exactly 2 ships.
Only operational ships of the appropriate type can be assigned to the various Task Forces.   Badly damaged ships or ships taken offline for repairs, upgrades, or conversions are not available.   However, all surface ships except those taken offline can be added to an escort Task Force.  In addition, a special evacuation rule allows offline ships to be added to Escort TFs if they are not too badly damaged (i.e. in danger of sinking) or if the enemy is about to capture the base.  The formation of the evacuation TF is automatic at the time of base capture.   Offline ships added to escort TFs will also incur additional damage - the assumption being normal repairs are interrupted.
6.1.2 Task Force Information Screen
Clicking on a Task Force brings up the Task Force Information Screen. The left side of this screen displays TF data, while the upper center and right side are for giving orders. In the list at center are the ships that make up the Task Force.


6.1.2.1 TF Destination
6.1.2.1.1 Set TF Destination
The Set TF Destination option allows the player to determine a destination hex for the current TF. To change the TF’s Destination Hex, click the arrow to the left of this title. In the above example, Task Force 405’s current destination hex is 137,101. To cancel this action, click the right mouse button before selecting a destination hex.
6.1.2.1.2 Set TF Routing
The Set TF Routing option allows the player to determine how the TF will move.  To Set TF Routing, click the arrow to the left of this title. The following screen will appear.



The Set TF Routing option allows the player to set the current TF to follow another TF, meet another TF, patrol within a set of boundary hexes, or define the path the TF will take to a set destination, by setting Waypoints.  The Set TF Routing option also allows the player to select the safety level of the routing path determined by the AI. The Safety levels are:
    • Normal - defines a normal routing path, 
    • Safest - defines a routing path that completely avoids all known enemy air concentrations,
    • Safer - defines a routing path that avoids known significant enemy air concentrations and
    • Direct - forces a direct routing path to the destination, regardless of any enemy air concentrations.
    • Coastal – forces a coastal routing path which can be a much safer route.

The initial default is “Normal” but this default may be changed by the player.   The new default can be set on any screen that allows the value for a given TF to be changed.
If the TF Routing of a TF is changed to “Follow Task Force” or “Meet Task Force”, or Waypoints or a Patrol Zone are set, a brief description of the setting is displayed below the “Set TF Routing” button on the Task Force Information Screen.
6.1.2.1.3 Threat Tolerance
This function allows the player to specify a TF’s Tolerance for detected enemy threats that could cause the TF to auto-retreat. Tolerance has four levels:
    • Normal – No offsets to retreat calculation.
    • Low – Lower threat tolerance = Higher chance of retreat from detected threats.
    • High – Higher threat tolerance = Lower chance of retreat from detected threats.
    • Absolute – Absolute threat tolerance = TF will never retreat from detected threats.

If the Threat Tolerance of a TF is changed from Normal, this is displayed in the text below the “Set TF Routing” button on the Task Force Information Screen:
    • If there is no TF Routing option (Follow Task Force, meet Task Force, Waypoints or Patrol Zone) selected the Threat Tolerance value is shown in full.
    • If a TF Routing option is set, the Threat Tolerance setting is added to the end of the TF Routing text: (-) for Low, (+) for High, or (!!) for Absolute.
6.1.2.1.4 Follow and Meet TF Orders
Both of these order one TF to track the movement of another.  There is an important difference in the implementation of the two methods:
    • Follow is designed for TFs that begin in the same general location and move together to a destination.   The following TF will fall in behind the followed TF and the followed TF will slow down if necessary to let the following TF keep up.
    • Meet is designed for TFs that begin at different locations and set paths of intercept at some mid-point.  The meeting TFs continuously adjusts its path to reach a meeting point.
6.1.2.1.4.1 Follow TF Orders


This option orders one TF to follow another to its destination.  The distance by which the following TF will trail the followed TF can be specified (zero is valid) and the following TF can be ordered to stand off from the followed TF at destination.
In the above example, Task Force 406 is currently not following another TF. To set it to Follow TF, click the arrow to the left of this title. The screen will be replaced by a large display of the tactical map; scroll around and click on a TF for the current one to follow. To cancel this action, click the right mouse button before selecting a TF to follow. Since it is not following another TF at this time, the TF Followed field displays ‘None’. Otherwise, the TF number being followed would be displayed here.
6.1.2.1.4.2 Meet TF Orders


The Meet TF option allows a TF to meet another selected TF in open water. To set TF 406 to meet another, click the arrow to the left of this title. The screen will be replaced by a large display of the tactical map; scroll around and click on a TF for the current one to meet.  Once they meet, the player may choose subsequent actions from a list of options: Meet then: Refuel the TF, Merge with the TF, or Follow the TF.  This option is especially useful for replenishment TFs.  The Distance option can be set to the number of hexes the TF will trail the chosen TF if the Follow option is selected and the two TFs have met.
6.1.2.1.5 Use Waypoints

The Use Waypoints option allows a TF to define its course to a destination, by using waypoints.  The Use Waypoints option is only available when a destination is set for the TF.  Three waypoints are available, and each may be set by clicking the arrow to the left of this title. The screen will be replaced by a large display of the tactical map; scroll around and click on a hex in order to set that waypoint. To cancel this action, click the right mouse button before selecting a waypoint hex.
6.1.2.1.6 Set Patrol Zone



The Set Patrol Zone option allows a TF to define the area in which it will patrol. The Set Patrol Zone option is only available when a destination for the TF is not set.  Clicking the arrow to the left of this title brings up two patrol options: Set Boundary, and Patrol Around Target. 
6.1.2.1.6.1 Set Boundary
Three Set Boundary options allows the player to patrol within a single hex: Set Boundary 1, only, patrol along a line; Set Boundary 1 and Set Boundary 2, and patrol along the perimeter of an area; Set Boundary 1, 2, and 3. Clicking the arrow to the left of each of these titles causes the screen to be replaced by a large display of the tactical map; scroll around and click on a hex in order to set that boundary.
6.1.2.1.6.2 Patrol Around Target
Patrol Around Target allows the player to identify a target hex, about which the TF will patrol, until the patrol order is cancelled.  The patrol will be set on the “enemy” side of the designated hex.
Once each patrol boundary hex is chosen, the player may set the number of days the TF will linger at the boundary hex.  This is very useful when a replenishment TF, for example, has orders to meet the TF in a designated hex. In this case, the patrolling TF may elect to refuel, when present in that hex.


6.1.3 Ship Information Screen
To get information about any of the various ships in a Task Force, or in a Port, click on the name of the ship (the same goes for Air and Land screens as well; click on the unit’s name to open up a more detailed screen). 



For the CV Lexington, the crew is not that great at Day fighting (with an experience of 48), but would be very poor at Night (with an experience of 34). The ship’s Captain, RADM Sherman, F., has a Leadership rating of 65 and an Inspiration rating of 69.



Lexington’s weapons are mostly defensive in nature. We can see that she has eight 8-inch gun batteries. Each weapon device is broken down as follows:
    • A number (Num) of devices of that type
    • A facing (Face), indicating the direction that this grouping of devices is facing. 
    • Mount indicates the maximum number of weapons that comprise the device. 
    • Armor details how well-protected the mount is from attack and/or damage (only the 8-inch guns have any protection at all)
    • Range shows in yards how far the device can fire
    • Pen shows how the weapon is rated at penetrating enemy armor
    • Ammo shows how well-stocked with ammunition that particular set of weapons is. If this number reaches zero, it will not provide any protection or offensive ability; a dry gun is a useless gun. 



Since Lexington is an Aircraft Carrier, she has an air component attached to her. U.S. carriers designated their squadrons with a “V” followed by the first letter of their function (F for Fighter, S for Scout, B for Dive Bomber, and T for Torpedo). A dash and number further identified the squadron. These squadrons were often called by their function and number (for example, “VF-1” would be called “Fighter One”).
We can see that Lexington has five total squadrons: VF-2 (Fighter Two) with 22 F2A-2 Buffalos, VS-2 (Scouting Two) with 18 SBD Dauntlesses, VB-2 (Bombing Two) with 18 SBD Dauntlesses, VT-2 (Torpedo Two) with 12 TBD Devastators and a Marine Bombing Squadron, VMSB-231, with 18 Vindicators. Lexington therefore is currently carrying 22 fighters, 54 dive bombers, and 12 torpedo bombers – 88 aircraft in all.



In the Unit Information Screen for the Lexington, we see that her Max Speed is 33 knots (equating in game turns to 9 hexes per turn – the number in parenthesis). Her Cruise Speed is 15 knots (equating to 4 hexes per turn). The carrier’s Maneuver rating is low at 26, meaning she can’t exactly turn on a dime like a nimble destroyer could. Her Anti-Aircraft value, essentially a measure of her anti-air strength, is 1036, and she has no anti-submarine warfare capabilities so her Anti-Submarine shows None.
The Lexington’s Endurance is 9780 and her Fuel is 5313. Both these numbers are indicated in a green color, showing that her supply for both these items is good. As they dwindle, the color-coding will change from yellow (depleted) to red (almost gone). 
Tonnage is used by the game to determine a ship’s construction costs, how many ships may be “docked” at ports of various sizes, how much of a port’s “repair” capacity will be used to repair damage or upgrade or convert the ship, and the like.
In this case the ship Tonnage is 36,000, which is similar to her listed “Standard” tonnage.  This will not be the case for auxiliary vessels or cargo ships.
Next is listed her Durability is an overall measure of the ship’s seaworthiness, and is at 126.
The ship’s Belt Armor, essentially the thickness of her hull at and below the waterline, is 175. Her Deck Armor, measuring the thickness of armor covering her topside deck, is 50. The Tower Armor rating of 50 shows that the aircraft carrier’s island structure (or central structure in other vessels) is as well-protected as her deck. 
The System Damage, Flood Damage, Engine Damage and Fires ratings are, thankfully for the Lexington’s crew, all at 0 right now. Again, these numbers are color-coded; the ’0’ is in green because this is an ideal setting; they can change to yellow (indicating damage) and red (indicating severe damage).
Lexington’s Aircraft Capacity is shown as 90 / 88, which means her maximum capacity is 90 but only has 88 aircraft on board (as we saw a few paragraphs ago). Also, her ability to conduct sorties is measured by Max Sorties, which we see is set at 414 / 414. The left-hand number indicates the maximum number of sorties able to fly, while the right-hand number dwindles as Lexington launches them. This number is added with other carrier’s Max Sorties ratings and totaled in the Task Force Information Screen’s total.
Next is listed the number of torpedoes available to be carried by aircraft capable of carrying torpedoes.
Also, Lexington’s Victory Value is 396, which is the number of points scored for the Japanese when this ship is sunk. 



At the bottom of the Lexington’s screen are several orders that can be given:
    • Scuttle, which sinks the ship and is only available if the ship is suffering from serious damage.
    • Next Ship, which will change the information on the screen to reflect the next ship (or previous ship, depending on the arrow clicked) in the TF.
    • Back, which will move the display back one screen.
    • Exit, which will exit to the Tactical Map.
The option to return to a major off map port (Return to Pearl Harbor) is only available in small map scenarios. It is only selectable when the player’s ships are in one of the designated main bases for the scenario. If not in a main base, this option is greyed out. In full map scenarios this “off-map” movement is not an option and will not be on the display. The number in parenthesis next to the “Return to Pearl Harbor” indicates the round trip travel time for this ship (in days) including time to repair or upgrade the ship once it reaches the off map port.



An additional set of new entries, in the Unit Information section, is the ship’s capacity to carry Troops, Bulk Cargo, and Liquid Cargo.  The capacity for Liquid Cargo represents a ship’s deep tanks, for example, which can carry raw liquid cargo such as fuel.  This is in addition to a ship’s ability to carry fuel stored in barrels, as bulk cargo, at a penalty capacity rate.
This ship can carry 75 troop points, as well as 75 cargo points.  The cargo points represent the ability to carry supplies and certain support weapons, such as Lt. Artillery, AA guns, mortars, etc.  This ship does not have the ability to carry liquid cargo.
And finally, we see that an upgrade for the ship can be made on or after 2/42 with No indicating that the player does not wish this upgrade to occur. Toggling this field to Yes will cause the upgrade to occur at the first available opportunity.  
6.1.3.1 Upgrades and Conversions
Upgrades and Conversions are similar in many ways but differ in one important respect.  The AI is only able to upgrade a ship; it is not able to perform a Conversion.  Conversions are player options and, as such, are not available to the AI.  



Ships may Upgrade or Convert by clicking on the highlighted Upgrade or Convert field on the Ship Information Screen. Clicking on either field will bring up a screen that shows the characteristics of the ship being Upgraded or Converted to.  There is an additional conversion option available to the Japanese player, an “AK Carry Troops” option.



Upgrade:  Clicking on the Upgrade field brings up an Upgrade screen that shows the characteristics of the ship type of the next allowable upgrade. A Next Upgrade button allows the player to cycle through all the upgrades available to the ship.  A Unit Information section, at the right of the screen, shows the unit information of the target ship, as well as the Upgrade timing, damage and yard requirements.  When a ship is being Upgraded or Converted, it is taken off-line and will not appear in the active ship list for the port. 



Upgrade System Damage:  is the amount of system degradation that will occur in the course of the upgrade/conversion.
Upgrade Engineering Damage:  is the amount of engineering degradation that will occur during the upgrade/conversion and is a function of the difference between the speed of the original and the upgraded/converted ship.
Upgrade Flotation Damage:  is the amount of flotation degradation that will occur during the upgrade/conversion and is a function of the difference between the original and the upgraded/converted durability values.
Upgrade Delay:  is the minimum time, in days, the ship will be taken offline in order to affect the upgrade/conversion. The ship will remain offline for this number of days, even if all damage has been repaired.
Shipyard Size: is the minimum Repair Yard size necessary to affect the upgrade/conversion.  When this value is “0”, upgrades/conversions can be made at Ports with a sufficient “ability” rating, as explained in the Port Section, below.



Conversion:  The Conversion field shows the ship ‘types’ that the current ship may convert to. Clicking on the Conversion field brings up a Conversion screen that shows the characteristics of the ship types of the ‘Convert to’ options; clicking on the yellow highlight for each ship type, brings up the characteristics for that type.  Conversions have the same damage, delay, and shipyard requirements as Upgrades.  However, they are not available to the AI. In the image above, we see that the xAKL Ogashima can convert into 4 ship types. They are PB, AG, ACM and AMc.



AK Carry Troops: In the lower left there is an option to convert cargo space to carry troops. Clicking on the “Convert Cargo Space to Carry Troops” button will allow certain Japanese cargo ships to convert 1/3 of their cargo carrying capacity directly into troop carrying capacity. This option does not require the ship to be in a repair yard, but the ship must be in a Port of level 6 or greater, this conversion will take at least five days to complete. Ships able to use this feature are Japanese merchant ships with:
    • Cargo Capacity of 3000 or greater;
    • System Damage of 20 or less;
    • Combined Engine and Flotation Damage of 10 or less; and
    • In a Port of Level 6 or greater.
6.2 Creating a Task Force
To create a Task Force, click on a base (either on the Tactical Map or the List All Bases Screen) and then the Form New Task Force option. You will then see the Task Force Creation Screen. There are a number of Primary Missions as well as several secondary ones (or, Roles).  Most TFs may contain a maximum of 25 ships (although 15 or less is most efficient for a combat TF) except for Escort and Transport TFs, which have a maximum of 100 ships. ASW and some other TFs are restricted to less than 25 ships.
Right clicking on a ship’s name in the Form/Transfer TF display will show details of the ship.  Every new TF created has:
    • Its home base defaulted to the port at which the TF was created. 
    • Its control defaulted to human control (except Auto Convoy TFs, and TFs created by the computer).
    • Its Patrol/Retreat Status defaulted to Retirement Allowed. For Air, Surface, and Submarine combat TFs, Max React Range is set to zero (reaction is not available for non-combat TFs.
The Mission types and the ships that can make up task forces with those Missions are listed in section 20.1.2.1. The abbreviations for ship types are standard U.S. Navy terminology, augmented by British and Japanese designations and are defined in that section.  
Many of these Missions are self-explanatory; common sense will tell you that an aircraft carrier would not be in a Sub Patrol task force. But others are more complicated; for example, Transport Missions can accommodate most warship types because the freighters need an escort. Yet a TF with an Air Combat Mission cannot contain transport ships, because no sane carrier skipper is going to sail into battle with a bunch of slow freighters clinging to his heels.
The ships that will be in a TF can be automatically selected using Auto Selection, or players can select the ships themselves. If there aren’t enough ships present to create a complete Task Force automatically, the new TF won’t be created. However, you can still select ships manually.  Only ships that can be in a task force will be displayed in the selection menu. Task forces that consist of more than 15 ships suffer diminishing returns in effectiveness defending against air attack and fighting in surface battles.
6.2.1 Assigning Missions
From the Task Force Creation Screen, you may select TF Missions. A TF may change its Mission at any time, although the new Mission must be appropriate for all the ships in the TF.
6.2.2 Disbanding Task Forces
